But I'm chewing normally, taking my time. It's almost like a slow, deliberate sort of self-sabotage, if that makes any sense. I'm just standing there, chewing, feeling terribly guilty with every bite, but I don't feel like I'm racing through it. So no, I haven't really noticed myself eating any faster than I would at a normal Tuesday night dinner.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you often find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Well... yes, I suppose I do. It’s not that I eat until I’m physically sick, you understand. I’ve never been one to make myself ill like that, thank goodness. I have a rather strong constitution, actually, always have. But... yes, I do reach a point where it’s just... too much. \n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen, like I said, just grazing away at the chicken or the cheese, and then maybe I’ll find a half-empty bag of those little chocolate candies my cousin likes to keep around—the ones with the peanut butter inside, she buys them by the sackful—and I’ll start in on those. And it’s only after I’ve been at it for an hour or so that I suddenly realize how my body is feeling. \n\nIt’s usually my belt that gives it away first. I wear these woven leather belts, very sturdy, but they don't have much give to them. And suddenly I’ll notice that it’s digging into my stomach, just feeling incredibly tight and restrictive. And the physical sensation in my stomach... it’s this heavy, bloated feeling. It’s not a sharp pain, but it’s a very distinct, very deep discomfort. It gets to the point where I can’t even comfortably walk back to the den. I have to go out to the garage—I have a little woodworking shop set up out there, nothing fancy, just a workbench and a few tools, and this old, battered armchair I rescued from a thrift store years ago. I have to go sit in that chair for a good long while, just leaning back, trying to let my stomach settle. \n\nI’ll just sit there in the dark, sometimes for thirty or forty minutes, feeling this awful, stretched-out sensation in my midsection, just waiting for the discomfort to pass enough so I can finally go to bed. It’s a miserable feeling, Doctor, truly miserable. You’d think the physical discomfort alone would be enough to stop me from doing it again the next night, but... well, here we are. So yes, to answer your question, I do eat until I am very, very uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, do you find yourself eating these nighttime meals in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: In secret... yes. I suppose secret is exactly the right word for it. It’s not something I’m proud of, Doctor, not in the slightest. \n\nYou see, my cousin, she usually goes up to bed around ten o'clock. She likes to watch her evening mystery programs, and then she turns in for the night. And I... well, I wait. I sit there in the living room, maybe reading a bit or just staring at the wall, waiting until I know for an absolute fact that the house is completely quiet and she is fast asleep. Sometimes, if I think she might be restless or if I just feel too exposed even in the dim light of the kitchen, I’ll actually take the food out to the garage. I mentioned my little woodworking shop out there earlier, didn't I? I’ll gather up the tupperware or the crackers and carry them out there to that old armchair, just to be absolutely certain I'm completely out of sight. \n\nAnd it is entirely because I would be so incredibly embarrassed for her, or really anyone in my family, to see how much I’m eating. The sheer quantity of it, it's just shameful. You have to understand my position in the family, Doctor. I have always been the disciplined one. Always. Even when I was a much younger man, I was the rock, the one who kept things organized and made the sensible decisions. My relatives have always looked at me as the responsible figure, the man who has a firm grip on himself and his life. \n\nSo, the thought of my cousin walking in, flipping on that bright overhead kitchen light, and catching me standing there mindlessly gorging myself on leftovers and cheese... it would just be humiliating. It would completely shatter the respect they have for me, seeing me so entirely lacking in self-control. I just couldn't bear the look on her face. So I go to great lengths to make absolutely sure I am alone when I do it.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you ever find yourself eating things that aren’t food—like ice, paper, or other unusual substances—or do you tend to stick to regular food items?\n\nPatient: Paper? Ice? Oh, no, no. Goodness, no. I haven't ever done anything like that. I suppose I understand why you might feel the need to ask, given the... well, the strange and secretive nature of what I'm doing out there in the dark, but no, it is strictly regular food. \n\nI can't even imagine trying to eat a piece of paper. And ice? Well, at my age, chewing on ice is just asking for a cracked molar. I have a dental bridge on my lower left side that cost me a small fortune about five years ago, and my dentist would absolutely have my head if I started crunching on ice cubes. I have to be careful enough with hard nuts as it is! \n\nNo, when I'm standing there in the kitchen, or sitting out in that armchair in the garage, it's always just... well, groceries. Actual food. Like I mentioned before, it's the leftover roast chicken, the sharp cheddar from the deli, the crackers. Sometimes it's a bit of bread—my cousin, she buys this wonderful sourdough from a little bakery downtown, and I might tear off a good chunk of that and put some butter on it. Real butter, mind you, none of that margarine nonsense. I've never been able to stand the taste of margarine, even when they swore up and down it was healthier for you back in the eighties. \n\nSo, it's always proper food, things that are actually meant to be eaten and enjoyed. I still appreciate good quality food, Doctor. It’s just the... the sheer volume of it that’s the issue, and the compulsive way I go about eating it in the middle of the night. But I’ve never had the slightest urge to eat anything that wasn't actually a food item. I suppose I still have my culinary standards, even if my self-control has completely gone out the window. I just stick to the leftovers and whatever is sitting in the pantry.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you've eaten very little or nothing at all during the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s quite the opposite, actually. I have always believed in three square meals a day. It’s how I was raised, you know, back when people actually sat down at a table instead of eating out of paper bags in their cars. \n\nI eat quite sensibly during the daytime. Breakfast is usually something sturdy, maybe a bowl of steel-cut oats—I take the time to make them properly on the stove, none of that instant microwave nonsense, it just turns to paste if you rush it—and perhaps a sliced banana or half a grapefruit. Then I'll have a modest lunch, maybe a half-sandwich or a bowl of soup around one o'clock. \n\nAnd then, of course, there is dinner. My cousin and I, and sometimes her son if he happens to drop by after his shift, we always sit down for a proper meal together around six-thirty. She is quite a traditional cook, very hearty. Just the other night she made this wonderful pot roast with carrots and those little red potatoes, cooked low and slow all afternoon so the meat just falls apart on your fork. We eat very well, Doctor. \n\nSo, by the time the evening winds down, I am completely satisfied. That's the most confounding part of it all. A lot of the time when these nighttime episodes happen, I’m not even hungry. Not in the slightest. My stomach isn't rumbling, I don't feel weak or empty, because I already had a very good, filling dinner with everyone just a few hours prior. I shouldn't need another bite of food until breakfast. \n\nBut despite that... despite knowing full well I don't need it, later on I still find myself out there in the quiet, opening containers and just tasting one thing after another. A bite of the leftover roast, a slice of cheese, a handful of crackers. It’s not driven by hunger at all. It's just this compulsion to keep tasting, keep chewing, even though my body has already had plenty of nourishment for the day.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you feel like you've lost control over your eating—like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that is precisely it. That feeling of losing control is exactly what brings me in to see you, Doctor. It is the most distressing part of this whole ordeal. \n\nYou see, I will be standing there in the kitchen, or sitting out in the garage like I told you, and I’ll take that first bite. Just a small piece of the cheddar, or a single slice of that sourdough bread. And in my mind, I fully intend for it to be just that one bite. But once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like there is this complete disconnect between my mind and my body. The sensible part of me—the rational man who has spent sixty-five years being responsible and disciplined—is right there in my head, clear as day, saying, \"That’s enough, Alejandro. You’ve had a taste, now put the lid back on the container and go to bed.\" \n\nI hear that voice perfectly. I agree with it! But my hand... my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It is as if my arm has suddenly developed a mind of its own. I will be looking right at the Tupperware, knowing full well I am not hungry, knowing that I should just close the refrigerator door and walk away, and yet I am reaching in for another piece of the roast chicken. And then maybe a cracker to go with it. And then another slice of cheese. \n\nIt’s a terrible, helpless feeling. To be a grown man, a man who has always prided himself on his self-restraint, and to be entirely unable to stop my own hand from reaching for more food... it’s just baffling. I keep chewing and swallowing, even while my brain is practically begging me to stop. I just can't seem to make myself halt the process once the lid is off and I've started. So, yes, \"losing control\" is exactly how I would describe it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Depressed? Hopeless? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I certainly get frustrated with myself over this... this lack of control with the eating, as I was just saying. It bothers me immensely. But it doesn't drag me down into a depression or make me feel hopeless about life in general. 

I still very much enjoy my day-to-day routine. I'm retired now, as you know, and I find a lot of satisfaction in my days. I still get up early, I read the morning paper—the actual physical paper, mind you, I still have it delivered because I can't stand trying to read articles on a little glowing screen—and I enjoy my coffee. I still tinker out in the garage, fixing up old radios. That’s been a hobby of mine since I was a young man in my twenties, back when things were actually built to last and you could repair them if they broke, instead of just throwing them away. I still find a great deal of pleasure in taking a broken piece of equipment, finding the faulty wire or the blown tube, and bringing it back to life. 

And I still enjoy spending time with my cousin and her family. We play cards on Sunday afternoons, usually Pinochle or Rummy, and I look forward to that every week. I haven't lost interest in any of those things. I still laugh at a good joke, I still appreciate a beautiful sunny afternoon sitting on the porch. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It’s really just this one specific, very baffling behavior in the middle of the night that is causing me distress. The rest of my life, my mood, my interests... they're all just fine, really. It’s just the eating that has me so turned around.

The sensible part of me—the rational man who has spent sixty-five years being responsible and disciplined—is right there in my head, clear as day, saying, \"That’s enough, Alejandro. I will be looking right at the Tupperware, knowing full well I am not hungry, knowing that I should just close the refrigerator door and walk away, and yet I am reaching in for another piece of the roast chicken. And then maybe a cracker to go with it. And then another slice of cheese. \n\nIt’s a terrible, helpless feeling. To be a grown man, a man who has always prided himself on his self-restraint, and to be entirely unable to stop my own hand from reaching for more food... it’s just baffling. I keep chewing and swallowing, even while my brain is practically begging me to stop. I just can't seem to make myself halt the process once the lid is off and I've started. I'll be out tending to my roses, or taking my sensible afternoon walk, and instead of enjoying the fresh air, I'm just ruminating on it. I'm thinking, 'Alejandro, you are a grown man, you've lived 65 years, why can't you just control yourself around a piece of cold chicken?' It makes me feel so foolish and out of control. It doesn't stop me from going about my day, no, I still get my errands done and what have you, but it certainly dampens my mood and makes me feel quite down on myself. It's just a constant, nagging disappointment that I carry around all day until the evening comes, and then the whole cycle threatens to start over again.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, when these nighttime eating episodes happen, do you usually wake up from sleep first and then start eating, or are you already awake and then you decide to go to the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Well, that's an interesting question, Doctor. You know, I've heard about people who do that—who actually wake up from a dead sleep, completely unconscious of what they're doing, and just start eating. I saw a program on the television once about a woman who would make entire sandwiches while she was practically still dreaming! But no, no, that's not me at all. I am very much awake. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's really about my schedule being so loose these days. I'll just be sitting in my armchair in the den—it's a very comfortable chair, brown leather, I've had it for years—and the television is on, maybe I'm watching a late-night talk show or a documentary, and I'm just fully awake. I haven't even been to bed yet. I'm just sitting there, and the house is so quiet, and that's when the thought creeps in. It starts as just a little inkling, you know, just a 'maybe I'll go see what's in the kitchen' kind of thought. I'm entirely conscious the whole time. I walk into the kitchen, I turn on the light, I open the refrigerator door... I'm awake for every single second of it. \n\nI almost wish I was asleep, to be perfectly honest with you! It might make me feel a little less guilty if I could blame it on sleepwalking or something like that. But no, I am wide awake, fully aware that I'm doing it, and fully aware that I shouldn't be doing it, which just makes the whole thing that much more frustrating. I'll just be up late, unable to settle down for the night, and that's when the whole ordeal begins. I've never once woken up from sleep to do it. It's always before I've even tried to go to bed.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, in the mornings after these nighttime eating episodes, do you find that you have very little appetite for breakfast?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... I suppose I haven't really paid too much attention to that specific detail, Doctor. Let me think. Breakfast has always been a rather sacred meal for me, you see. Ever since I was a young man, my father instilled in me the importance of starting the day with a solid foundation. 'You can't build a house on sand, Alejandro,' he used to say, usually while he was frying up some eggs and chorizo. So, I've always been one to have a proper breakfast—a bit of oatmeal, perhaps some toast with marmalade, a good strong cup of coffee. \n\nNow, on the mornings after one of these... episodes... well, I can't say for certain that my appetite is completely gone. I mean, I still go through the motions, I still put the kettle on and get the oatmeal out. It's so ingrained in my routine, you know? But am I actually hungry for it? Or am I just eating it because it's 8:00 AM and that's what I've done every day for the last forty years? I'm honestly not sure. I suppose my stomach does feel rather full and heavy, like I mentioned before, just sort of sluggish... so maybe the actual physical hunger isn't quite there the way it is on a normal morning. But I couldn't say definitively that I have *no* appetite. I just sort of... eat my breakfast anyway, because it's breakfast time, even if I'm still feeling the physical and emotional weight of whatever I ate at two in the morning. It's a bit of a blur, to be honest, those mornings. I'm usually so distracted by feeling disappointed in myself that I don't really stop to assess my actual hunger levels.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than usual?\n\nPatient: Rapidly? Well, now... that’s something I really have to stop and picture. You know, when you’re in the middle of it, you aren't exactly timing yourself with a stopwatch. But if I really put myself back in that moment—standing there in the kitchen, usually just by the light of the little bulb over the stove because I don't want to turn the main overheads on and wake my cousin... I don't think I'm eating particularly fast. No, I wouldn't say it's rapid. \n\nYou see, because I'm already awake, and there's absolutely nowhere I need to be, there isn't any rush. It's not like when I was a younger man working and I had fifteen minutes to choke down a sandwich before a meeting. It's actually quite the opposite. I'll take out the tupperware—let's say it's some leftover roast chicken from Sunday dinner—and I'll just stand there, pulling it apart piece by piece. I might eat a piece, then maybe I'll open the cheese drawer and cut a few slices of sharp cheddar... I'm very particular about my cheese, Doctor, I get this wonderful aged cheddar from a little deli down the street. Anyway, I'll have a slice of that, maybe a cracker or two. \n\nSo, it's more of a... a continuous, steady grazing, I suppose you could call it. It’s the sheer amount of it that’s the problem, not the speed. I just keep going back for another bite, and another, and before I know it, half the chicken is gone and I've made a terrible dent in a sleeve of Ritz crackers. But I'm chewing normally, taking my time. It's almost like a slow, deliberate sort of self-sabotage, if that makes any sense. I'm just standing there, chewing, feeling terribly guilty with every bite, but I don't feel like I'm racing through it. So no, I haven't really noticed myself eating any faster than I would at a normal Tuesday night dinner.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you often find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Well... yes, I suppose I do. It’s not that I eat until I’m physically sick, you understand. I’ve never been one to make myself ill like that, thank goodness. I have a rather strong constitution, actually, always have. But... yes, I do reach a point where it’s just... too much. \n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen, like I said, just grazing away at the chicken or the cheese, and then maybe I’ll find a half-empty bag of those little chocolate candies my cousin likes to keep around—the ones with the peanut butter inside, she buys them by the sackful—and I’ll start in on those. And it’s only after I’ve been at it for an hour or so that I suddenly realize how my body is feeling. \n\nIt’s usually my belt that gives it away first. I wear these woven leather belts, very sturdy, but they don't have much give to them. And suddenly I’ll notice that it’s digging into my stomach, just feeling incredibly tight and restrictive. And the physical sensation in my stomach... it’s this heavy, bloated feeling. It’s not a sharp pain, but it’s a very distinct, very deep discomfort. It gets to the point where I can’t even comfortably walk back to the den. I have to go out to the garage—I have a little woodworking shop set up out there, nothing fancy, just a workbench and a few tools, and this old, battered armchair I rescued from a thrift store years ago. I have to go sit in that chair for a good long while, just leaning back, trying to let my stomach settle. \n\nI’ll just sit there in the dark, sometimes for thirty or forty minutes, feeling this awful, stretched-out sensation in my midsection, just waiting for the discomfort to pass enough so I can finally go to bed. It’s a miserable feeling, Doctor, truly miserable. You’d think the physical discomfort alone would be enough to stop me from doing it again the next night, but... well, here we are. So yes, to answer your question, I do eat until I am very, very uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, do you find yourself eating these nighttime meals in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: In secret... yes. I suppose secret is exactly the right word for it. It’s not something I’m proud of, Doctor, not in the slightest. \n\nYou see, my cousin, she usually goes up to bed around ten o'clock. She likes to watch her evening mystery programs, and then she turns in for the night. And I... well, I wait. I sit there in the living room, maybe reading a bit or just staring at the wall, waiting until I know for an absolute fact that the house is completely quiet and she is fast asleep. Sometimes, if I think she might be restless or if I just feel too exposed even in the dim light of the kitchen, I’ll actually take the food out to the garage. I mentioned my little woodworking shop out there earlier, didn't I? I’ll gather up the tupperware or the crackers and carry them out there to that old armchair, just to be absolutely certain I'm completely out of sight. \n\nAnd it is entirely because I would be so incredibly embarrassed for her, or really anyone in my family, to see how much I’m eating. The sheer quantity of it, it's just shameful. You have to understand my position in the family, Doctor. I have always been the disciplined one. Always. Even when I was a much younger man, I was the rock, the one who kept things organized and made the sensible decisions. My relatives have always looked at me as the responsible figure, the man who has a firm grip on himself and his life. \n\nSo, the thought of my cousin walking in, flipping on that bright overhead kitchen light, and catching me standing there mindlessly gorging myself on leftovers and cheese... it would just be humiliating. It would completely shatter the respect they have for me, seeing me so entirely lacking in self-control. I just couldn't bear the look on her face. So I go to great lengths to make absolutely sure I am alone when I do it.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you ever find yourself eating things that aren’t food—like ice, paper, or other unusual substances—or do you tend to stick to regular food items?\n\nPatient: Paper? Ice? Oh, no, no. Goodness, no. I haven't ever done anything like that. I suppose I understand why you might feel the need to ask, given the... well, the strange and secretive nature of what I'm doing out there in the dark, but no, it is strictly regular food. \n\nI can't even imagine trying to eat a piece of paper. And ice? Well, at my age, chewing on ice is just asking for a cracked molar. I have a dental bridge on my lower left side that cost me a small fortune about five years ago, and my dentist would absolutely have my head if I started crunching on ice cubes. I have to be careful enough with hard nuts as it is! \n\nNo, when I'm standing there in the kitchen, or sitting out in that armchair in the garage, it's always just... well, groceries. Actual food. Like I mentioned before, it's the leftover roast chicken, the sharp cheddar from the deli, the crackers. Sometimes it's a bit of bread—my cousin, she buys this wonderful sourdough from a little bakery downtown, and I might tear off a good chunk of that and put some butter on it. Real butter, mind you, none of that margarine nonsense. I've never been able to stand the taste of margarine, even when they swore up and down it was healthier for you back in the eighties. \n\nSo, it's always proper food, things that are actually meant to be eaten and enjoyed. I still appreciate good quality food, Doctor. It’s just the... the sheer volume of it that’s the issue, and the compulsive way I go about eating it in the middle of the night. But I’ve never had the slightest urge to eat anything that wasn't actually a food item. I suppose I still have my culinary standards, even if my self-control has completely gone out the window. I just stick to the leftovers and whatever is sitting in the pantry.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you've eaten very little or nothing at all during the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s quite the opposite, actually. I have always believed in three square meals a day. It’s how I was raised, you know, back when people actually sat down at a table instead of eating out of paper bags in their cars. \n\nI eat quite sensibly during the daytime. Breakfast is usually something sturdy, maybe a bowl of steel-cut oats—I take the time to make them properly on the stove, none of that instant microwave nonsense, it just turns to paste if you rush it—and perhaps a sliced banana or half a grapefruit. Then I'll have a modest lunch, maybe a half-sandwich or a bowl of soup around one o'clock. \n\nAnd then, of course, there is dinner. My cousin and I, and sometimes her son if he happens to drop by after his shift, we always sit down for a proper meal together around six-thirty. She is quite a traditional cook, very hearty. Just the other night she made this wonderful pot roast with carrots and those little red potatoes, cooked low and slow all afternoon so the meat just falls apart on your fork. We eat very well, Doctor. \n\nSo, by the time the evening winds down, I am completely satisfied. That's the most confounding part of it all. A lot of the time when these nighttime episodes happen, I’m not even hungry. Not in the slightest. My stomach isn't rumbling, I don't feel weak or empty, because I already had a very good, filling dinner with everyone just a few hours prior. I shouldn't need another bite of food until breakfast. \n\nBut despite that... despite knowing full well I don't need it, later on I still find myself out there in the quiet, opening containers and just tasting one thing after another. A bite of the leftover roast, a slice of cheese, a handful of crackers. It’s not driven by hunger at all. It's just this compulsion to keep tasting, keep chewing, even though my body has already had plenty of nourishment for the day.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, during these nighttime eating episodes, do you feel like you've lost control over your eating—like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that is precisely it. That feeling of losing control is exactly what brings me in to see you, Doctor. It is the most distressing part of this whole ordeal. \n\nYou see, I will be standing there in the kitchen, or sitting out in the garage like I told you, and I’ll take that first bite. Just a small piece of the cheddar, or a single slice of that sourdough bread. And in my mind, I fully intend for it to be just that one bite. But once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like there is this complete disconnect between my mind and my body. The sensible part of me—the rational man who has spent sixty-five years being responsible and disciplined—is right there in my head, clear as day, saying, \"That’s enough, Alejandro. You’ve had a taste, now put the lid back on the container and go to bed.\" \n\nI hear that voice perfectly. I agree with it! But my hand... my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It is as if my arm has suddenly developed a mind of its own. I will be looking right at the Tupperware, knowing full well I am not hungry, knowing that I should just close the refrigerator door and walk away, and yet I am reaching in for another piece of the roast chicken. And then maybe a cracker to go with it. And then another slice of cheese. \n\nIt’s a terrible, helpless feeling. To be a grown man, a man who has always prided himself on his self-restraint, and to be entirely unable to stop my own hand from reaching for more food... it’s just baffling. I keep chewing and swallowing, even while my brain is practically begging me to stop. I just can't seem to make myself halt the process once the lid is off and I've started. So, yes, \"losing control\" is exactly how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Alejandro, have you ever felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, especially around the time these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Hopeless? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I certainly get frustrated with myself over this... this lack of control with the eating, as I was just saying. It bothers me immensely. But it doesn't drag me down into a depression or make me feel hopeless about life in general. \n\nI still very much enjoy my day-to-day routine. I'm retired now, as you know, and I find a lot of satisfaction in my days. I still get up early, I read the morning paper—the actual physical paper, mind you, I still have it delivered because I can't stand trying to read articles on a little glowing screen—and I enjoy my coffee. I still tinker out in the garage, fixing up old radios. That’s been a hobby of mine since I was a young man in my twenties, back when things were actually built to last and you could repair them if they broke, instead of just throwing them away. I still find a great deal of pleasure in taking a broken piece of equipment, finding the faulty wire or the blown tube, and bringing it back to life. \n\nAnd I still enjoy spending time with my cousin and her family. We play cards on Sunday afternoons, usually Pinochle or Rummy, and I look forward to that every week. I haven't lost interest in any of those things. I still laugh at a good joke, I still appreciate a beautiful sunny afternoon sitting on the porch. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It’s really just this one specific, very baffling behavior in the middle of the night that is causing me distress. The rest of my life, my mood, my interests... they're all just fine, really. It’s just the eating that has me so turned around."
  }
